                        For 261 Rf(2) an α - energy of Eα = 8.51±0.01 MeV is measured. Half-lives for SF and α decay vary
                        considerably as seen in ﬁg. 15. For the SF events a half-life T1/2 = 2.57+0.66 −0.44 s is obtained, for the α
                        decays a value T1/2 = 5.95+2.07
                                                    −1.22 s, the combined   half-life of ﬁssion and α decays  is T1/2 = 3.80+0.72
                                                                                                                            −0.52 s.
                        It should be emphasized, that the half-lives obtained for the SF events is lower than the value obtained
                        from the α events.This excludes the possibility that the diﬀerent half-lives are due to an SF contribution
                        of 261 Rf(1). The time distributions for the ﬁssion (SF) events and α events are shown in ﬁg. 15a (SF
                        events), 15b (α events) and 15c (α + SF events). Although the diﬀerence by more than a factor of
                        two may suggest diﬀerent sources for the SF and α acticities, the low number of events and the broad
                        straggling of the decay times indicate that such an assumption is not necessarily straightforward. So we
                        presently assign both, α decays and SF events to the same activity and give a half-life T1/2 = 3.80+0.72 −0.52
                        s.
                        A detailed decay study of 261 Rf using α - γ - spectroscopy was performed by M. Asai [99], who used the
                        production reaction 248 Cm(18 O,5n)261 Rf. In this experiment only the long-lived activity 261 Rf(1) was
                        observed, no experimental evidence for 261 Rf(2), in-line with the results of previous studies [97, 98], but
                        in contradiction with the later study of H. Haba et al. [100], who reported for the same reaction nearly
                        equal production rates of 261 Rf(1) and 261 Rf(2). On the basis of α - γ coincidence measurements M. Asai
                        assigned 261 Rf(1) to the 9/2+ [615] Nilsson level (’most probable’) or the 11/2− [725] (’possible’) one and
                        interpreted it as the isomeric state.
                        The existence of a low lying isomeric state is expected from theoretical predictions. A. Parkhomenko and
                        A. Sobiczewski [35] predict the 11/2− [725] Nilsson level as the ground state of 261 Rf and the 3/2+ [622]
                        (E∗ = 50 keV), 7/2+ [613] (E∗ = 60 keV), 1/2+ [620] (E∗ = 80 keV) levels as low lying states at E∗ < 100
                        keV.
                        Given the assignment of M. Asai for 261 Rf(1), possible states for 261 Rf(2) would then be 3/2+ [622] or
                        1/2+ [620]. Such an assignment could also explain the high ﬁssion branch of 261 Rf(2) (bSF = 0.73±0.06
                        [100]) as commonly low spin states have lower ﬁssion barriers than high spin states in the same isotope.
                        (see e.g. [48]).

                                263
                               Sg
                        The isotope 263 Sg was ﬁrst synthesized by A. Ghiorso et al.[84] in the reaction 249 Cf(18 O,4n)263 Sg. They
                        observed a ’principal decay energy’ of Eα = 9.06±0.4 MeV and a weaker line at Eα = 9.25 MeV. For
                        both activities a common half-life of T1/2 = 0.9±0.2 s is given. The result was later conﬁrmed by K.E.
                        Gregorich et al. [85] and also in production by the reaction 238 U(30 Si,5n)263 Sg both α lines were observed
                        [111, 112].
                        The low energy activity was also observed when 263 Sg was produced by α decay of 267 Hs, synthesized in
                        the reaction 238 U(34 S,5n)267 Hs [113].
                        The situation appeared completely diﬀerent when 263 Sg was produced by α decay within the decay chain

                of 271 Ds (271 Ds α
                                  →
                                    267
                                        Hs α→
                                               263
                                                   Sg). In this case (except one case) only the Eα = 9.25±0.02 MeV line is
                observed [87, 88, 89, 90, 91]. It was so far straightforward to assume the existence of a long-lived isomeric
                state in 263 Sg. The half-life obtained from the recorded events is T1/2 = 0.33+0.10
                                                                                                 −0.06 s. From the decays of
                Eα < 9.15 MeV reported in [85, 111, 112, 113] one obtains Eα = 9.05±0.04 MeV and T1/2 = 0.79+0.37     −0.19 s,
                in agreement with the results reported in [84]. As it is presently unclear which α line represents the decay
                of the ground state and which the decay of the isomer, the 9.05-MeV activity will be denoted as 263 Sg(1)
                and the 9.25-MeV activity as 263 Sg(2). Both α transitions are unhindered. Using the half-life formula
                suggested by D.N. Poernaru et al. [92], one obtains Tα = 0.73 s for the 9.05-MeV activity and Tα =
                0.197 s for the 9.25-MeV activity. This indicates that they represent decays between analogous levels in
                the mother and daughter nuclei. The existence of an isomeric state is not unexpected from predicted
                low lying levels in 263 Sg [35] as seen in ﬁg. 16. The ground state is predicted as 11/2− [725], while the
                ﬁrst excited Nilsson level is 3/2+ [622]. Spin diﬀerence of ∆I = 4 (or even ∆I = 5, if the 1/2+ [620] level
                is located below the 3/2+ [622] one) suggests long halﬂives for internal transition (>>1 s) and thus the
                existence of an isomeric state decaying by α emission. The low lying level scheme predicted for 259 Rf
                (see ﬁg. 16) suggests also the existence of a longlived isomer in the daughter nucleus 259 Rf and explains
                the observed α - α correlations 263 Sg - 259 Rf (see sect. 5.7). As the α transitions are unhindered and
                internal transitions 11/2− [725] → 3/2+ [622] (1/2+ [620]) are unprobable due to the long lifetimes, one
                expects, assigning the 9.25-MeV transition to 263 Sg(2) only transitions 263 Sg(2) → 259 Rf(2).

                   265
                       Hs
                The isotope 265 Hs was ﬁrst observed in march 1984 in an irradiation of 208 Pb with 58 Fe at SHIP, GSI,
                which marked the discovery of element 108 (’hassium’) [114]. An alpha-decay energy of Eα = 10.36±0.03
                MeV and a half-life of T1/2 = 1.8+2.2
                                                  −0.7 ms were obtained. More detailed decay data were obtained in
                experiments at SHIP in 1994, 1997 [193, 197], 1999 and 2002. The sum of the results of all these ex-
                periments were presented in [164]. The analysis clearly shows the existence of two components in the
                complex α spectrum (see ﬁg. 17) and table 1.

                    The existence of an isomeric state can be understood on the basis of the level scheme predicted by A.
                Parkhomenko and A. Sobiczewski [35] (see ﬁg. 18). The ground state of 265 Hs is predicted as 11/2− [725],
                the ﬁrst excited state is 3/2+ [622] which was assigned as the ground state in 261 Sg [64], whereas according
                to the prediction the 3/2+ [622] and 1/2+ [620] states are degenerated in energy. So, one decay branch
                could be interpreted as 265 Hs (3/2+ [622]) → 261 Sg (3/2+ [622]), the other one as 265 Hs (11/2− [725])
                → 261 Sg (11/2− [725]). As the 11/2− [725] level, however, is predicted in 261 Sg at E∗ = 10 keV, due
                to the large angular momenta diﬀerence ∆L = 4,5 (and thus strong K hindrance) to the lower lying
                states it could be expected as a longlived isomeric state decaying by α emission. Alpha transitions 261 Sg

24


     (11/2− [725]) → 257 Rf (11/2− [725]), however, are not observed. In context to the 9µs isomer in 261 Sg it
     was suspected that contrary to the prediction the 11/2− [725] might be located above the 7/2+ [613] and a
     decay path 11/2− [725] M    2     +      E2     +
                              → 7/2 [613] → 3/2 [622] was considered (see sect. 5.7). The non-observation of
     an α transition between the 11/2− [725] states in 261 Sg and 257 Rf seems to corroborate this assumption.
     The interpretation of the α spectrum is not straightforward. A clear assignment to transitions between
     certain levels in the mother and daughter nuclei cannot be given on the quality of the present data. This
     has to be left for further studies. We will do here with giving some remarks to be taken into account for
     further studies, which have to show if they are conﬁrmed or ’falsiﬁed’.
     The theoretical half-life according to [92] for the Eα = 10538 keV - line is Tα = 0.31 ms, which results
     in a hindrance factor HF = 1, i.e. the decay represents an unhindered α transition between analogous
     states in 265 Hs and 261 Sg.
     Relative intensities (irel ) for the lines attributed to 265 Hs(1) are 0.78 for Eα = 10282/10312 keV - lines
     (below reasons why here these two lines are treated as one transition are given), 0.05 for the Eα = 10428
     keV - line, 0.17 for the Eα = 10573 keV - line. Theoretical half-lives [92] and hindrance factors are Tα =
     1.33 ms, HF = 1.83 for the Eα = 10282/10312 keV - lines, Tα = 0.57 ms, HF = 67 for the Eα = 10428
     keV - line, and Tα = 0.25 ms, HF = 45 for the Eα = 10573 keV - line using the relation HF = T1/2 / (irel
     × Tα ). So, the Eα = 10282/10312 keV - lines represent an unhindered α transition between analogous
     states in 265 Hs and 261 Sg; this could be the transition 265 Hs (11/2− [725]) → 261 Sg (11/2− [725]). Assum-
     ing the 11/2− [725] as an isomeric one at E∗ ≈ 200 keV, the Eα = 10538 keV - line could then represent
     the unhindered transition 265 Hs (3/2+ [622]) → 261 Sg (3/2+ [622]). As the 11/2− [725] state in 261 Sg was
     assumed to decay into the 7/2+ [613] one predominanty by internal conversion, the Eα = 10282 keV - line
     could represent the α decay into the 11/2− [725] level, while the Eα = 10312 keV - line could be the sum
     line Eα = 10312 keV + ECE (11/2− [725] → 7/2+ [613] ). Due to the lifetime of the 11/2− [725] level of
     13 µs the summing eﬀect would be rather small. An assignment of the Eα = 10428 keV and Eα = 10573
     keV - lines, however, is quite uncertain. One could assume direct α decay 265 Hs (11/2− [725]) α      →
                                                                                                              261
                                                                                                                  Sg
          +
     (7/2 [613]). Such a transition, however, requires a parity change, for which hindrance factors HF > 100
     are expected [115], which is in contradiction with the observed hindrance factors for these transitions.
     So an assignment has to be left for further studies.
     Decay of 265 Hs was recently also studied by N. Sato et al. [116] at the GARIS separator at RIKEN (Wako,
     Japan). The data, however, did not deliver deeper insight into the decay of 265 Hs(1) and 265 Hs(2).



     5.9 Isomers in N = 159 isotones

        265
             Sg
     The isotope 265 Sg was ﬁrst observed by Yu.A. Lazarev et al. [117] who produced it in the reaction
     248
         Cm(22 Ne,5n)265 Sg. They reported a broad α-energy distribution in the range Eα = (8.72 - 8.91) MeV.
     A half-life was not obtained. A few years later using the same reaction A. Türler et al. [101] principally
     conﬁrmed the results of [117]. They reported also a broad alpha-energy distribution Eα = (8.69 - 8.97)
     MeV, which could be subdivided into four groups (the numbers in brackets give the relative intensities),
     Eα1 = 8.94 MeV (0.23), Eα2 = 8.84 MeV (0.46), Eα3 = 8.76 MeV (0.23), Eα4 = 8.69 MeV (0.08), and a
     half-life of T1/2 = 7.4+3.3
                             −2.7 s. Diﬀerent decay data were observed, when
                                                                                    265
                                                                                        Sg was produced by α decay
         269
     of      Hs [102, 103, 104, 105, 106, 107, 108, 109]. The energy distribution of the α decays, observed in
     ’direct’ production was not reproduced in the ’indirect’ production (via α decay of 269 Hs); also in indirect
     production α decay of 265 Sg was followed to a large extent by spontaneous ﬁssion of 261 Rf, which was
     not the case in ’direct’ production. These observations indicated the existence of two longlived levels in
     265
         Sg which are populated with diﬀerent intensities in ’direct’ or ’indirect’ production.
     The data published up to 2008 were reexamined by Ch. Düllmann and A. Türler [118], who concluded
     each two longlived states in 265 Sg and 261 Rf. The result of their analysis is shown in ﬁg. 19 (upper part).
     265
         Sg(1) (Eα = (8.8-8.9) MeV, T1/2 = 8.9+2.7 −1.9 s) decays predominantly (irel = 0.8) into
                                                                                                      261
                                                                                                          Rf(1) (Eα =
                                                              265          261
     8.28 MeV, T1/2 = 68 s), with a small decay branch            Sg(1) →      Rf(2) (irel = 0.2), while 265 Sg(2) (Eα
     = 8.69MeV, T1/2 = 16.2+4.7 −3.5 s) decays predominantly (irel = 0.88) into
                                                                                    261
                                                                                        Rf(2) (Eα = 8.51 MeV, T1/2
                                          265           261
     = 3 s), with a small decay branch        Sg(2) →       Rf(1) (irel = 0.12).
     Another detailed decay study of 265 Sg was later performed by H. Haba et al. [110], which conﬁrmed the
     conclusions drawn in [118] (see ﬁg. 19, lower part).

         267
             Hs
     Evidence for the existence of an isomeric state in 267 Hs is reported by K. Morita et al. [89] on the basis
     of a single α decay chain observed in the reaction 64 Ni + 208 Pb leading to the evaporation residue 271 Ds.
     The chain consisted of three α particles interpreted as:
     271
         Ds (Eα = 10440 keV, ∆t = 46 ms) → 267 Hs (Eα = 9730 keV, ∆t = 1158.6 ms)→ 263 Sg (Eα = 9310 keV,
     ∆t = 5.999 s). Assuming, however, that the single event for 263 Sg (9.310 MeV) might be still in-line with
     the mean energy Eα = 9.25±0.02 MeV) the large discrepancy between the time diﬀerece and the half-life
     by a factor of 16 (probability <10−5 ) is still striking.

       Tab. 2: Hindrance factors for decays of 271 Ds (10.44 MeV), 267 Hs (9.73 MeV), 263 Sg (9.30 MeV)

 Isotope       Eα /MeV      ∆t / ms     T1/2 / ms      Tα / ms     Hf

       Ds      10.44        46          32             2.2         14.5

        The α energy of the mother event 271 Ds is extremely low (see sect. 5.10 for more exhaustive discussion),
        the α energy and lifetime of the second chain member is signiﬁcantly diﬀerent (for the lifetime a factor
        of 26) to the mean values for 267 Hs (see ﬁg. 20) and the α energy of 263 Sg is somewhat higher, the
        lifetime is considerably longer than the literature values reported for 263 Sg(1) and 263 Sg(2) (see sect.
        5.8). Remarkebly all three decays seem to be strongly hindered, as can be seen in table 2. Although it is
        quite problematic to estimate the half-life on the basis of one event as T1/2 = ln2×∆t as done here, but
        it should be noted that the ’experimental half-life’ is more than an order of magnitude longer than the
        calculated one [92, 93] in all three cases. Calculated level schemes for 271 Ds, 267 Hs and 263 Sg [35] are
        shown in ﬁg. 16. According to the calculations isomeric states in 271 Ds and 267 Hs can be expected if the
        1/2+ [620] level is placed below the 3/2+ [622] level which certainly not seems to be unrealistic, while in
        263
            Sg the 3/2+ [622] level is expected to be isomeric. Thus, provided, the 1/2+ [620] level in 271 Ds being
        isomeric the following α decay sequence is possible:
        271                                                IT (M 1) 263
            Ds (1/2+ ) α1
                       →
                          267
                              Hs (1/2+ ) α2
                                         →
                                            263
                                                Sg (1/2+ ) →            Sg (3/2+ ).
        However, in that scenario the decays α1 and α2 would be unhindered in contrast to the experimental
        ﬁnding. So the assignment of the (10.44 MeV/46 ms) - transition of 271 Ds is not straightforeward.
        Further experimental data are needed to clarify the situation.




         5.10 Isomers in N = 161 isotones

            267
                Sg
        The isotope 267 Sg has been so far only observed as α decay daughter of 271 Hs, produced in the reaction
        248
            Cm(26 Mg,3n)271 Hs by J. Dvorak et al. [108, 109] and as α decay granddaughter of 275 Ds, produced
        in the reaction 232 Th(48 Ca,5n)275 Ds by Yu. Ts. Oganessian et al. [119]. The results of the 248 Cm -
        irradiations were summarized in [109] as follows: Eα = (8.02±0.05) MeV, T1/2 = 80+60      −20 s, bα = 0.17,
        bsf =,0.83.
        The complete results are listed in table 3. The results of [109] were principally reproduced in [119] but
        interpreted in a diﬀerent way.
        The results of Yu.Ts. Oganessian clearly show two groups of α decays of 271 Hs, which is not so signiﬁcant
        in [108, 109] due to worse energy resolution (see sect. 5.11 for further discussion). The group of Eα >
        9.30 MeV is correlated to α decays of 267 Sg, that of Eα < 9.30 MeV is correlated to SF. Half-life for the
        α decays (Ch1,Ch6,Ch7,Ch8) is T1/2 = 465+465 −155 s. Theoretical half-life according to [92] is Tα = 300 s;
        thus the decay has to be regarded as unhindered.
        For the ﬁssion events a half-life T1/2 = 84+51                 ¶
                                                      −23 s is obtained , which suggests that α decays and SF
        represent diﬀerent activities.

            269
                Hs
        On the basis of two decay chains observed in irradiations of 238 U with 40 Ar and attributed the decay of
        the ground state of 273g Ds (11/2− [725]) and an isomeric state 273m Ds (1/2+ [620]) Yu.Ts. Oganessian
        et al. [119] postulated a 1/2+ [620] isomer in 269 Hs at E∗ ≈ 20 keV. As their postulation of an isomeric
        1/2+ [620] - state in 273 Ds is seemingly dubious (see sect. 5.11 for detailed discussion) the claim of an
        isomer 269 Hs seems presently dubious too.

            271
               Ds
        The only case in N = 161 even-Z isotones for which real evidence for an isomeric state was found so
        far is 271 Ds. It was ﬁrst produced at SHIP, GSI in the reaction 64 Ni + 208 Pb. Besides the assumed

        = 56+270

        [89]. Alpha - decay energies are plotted versus the decay times (time diﬀerence between implantation

           there is no real connection between the α energies and the time diﬀerences except for two cases reported
           by K. Morita et al. [89] (Eα = 10.44 MeV, ∆t(ER-α) = 46 ms; Eα = 10.33 MeV, ∆t(ER-α) = 87.1 ms).
           The latter event, however, was observed as a single event, i.e. no subsequent decays of 267 Hs, 263 Sg,
           259
               Rf, 255 No were observed, while for the ﬁrst event energies and time diﬀerences for 267 Hs (Eα = 9.73
           MeV, ∆t(ER-α) = 1158.6 ms), and 263 Sg (Eα = 9.310 MeV, ∆t(ER-α) = 5998.9 ms) diﬀer signiﬁcantly
           from the decay values of these isotopes in the other decay chains and α decay of 259 Rf or 255 No was not
           observed. Thus the assignment of both decay chains appears questionable. (Here only events with full
           energy release in the stop detector are considered due to worse energy resolution for ’sum events’). The
           time distribution of the events is shown in ﬁg. 21b; clearly a long lived component is visible and clearly
           separated from the ’short’ lived events. Taking all events ∆t < 12 ms as the ’short-lived’ component,
           one obtains a half-life T1/2 = 1.93+0.43                                                              +116
                                                −0.30 ms. For the long-lived component one obtains T1/2 = 85−31 ms
                                            +52
           taking all events, or T1/2 = 69−21 ms, disregarding the 10.44 MeV and 10.33 MeV events. Tentatively
           we assume a single energy for ’all’ events resulting in Eα = 10.74±0.01 MeV; the theoretical half-life for
           271
               Ds events at this energy is Tα = 0.44 ms according to the formula suggested by D.N. Poenaru et al.
           [92, 93]. The low energy level schemes calculated by A. Parkhomenko and A. Sobiczewski [35] are shown
           in ﬁg. 22. Provided the 1/2+ [620] Nilsson level lying below the 3/2+ [622] level, indeed due to an angular
           momentum diﬀerence ∆l = 3,4 to the lower lying 7/2+ [613] and 9/2+ [615] levels a low lying isomeric 1/2+
           state could be expected. However, such an isomer could decay via an unhindered α transition into the
           corresponding level in 267 Hs, as indicated by the dashed line in ﬁg. 22. This, however, is in contradiction
           with the long half-life of the isomeric state. Even for an α transition energy of 10.40 MeV one expects
           an α half-life of 2.85 ms [92, 93]. This, however, means, a 1/2+ isomer may exist in 271 Ds, but it is
           expected to have similar half-life (and probably also a similar α decay energy) as the ground state, but
           certainly is in conﬂict with the long lifetime. Altough the existence of an isomer in 271 Ds has to be
           regarded as certain, its conﬁguration is unclear. Thus it is tempting to speculate on the existence of a
           3-quasi-particle K isomer, decaying by internal transitions to the ground state.This would be the heavi-
           est K isomer observed so far. It thus seems of high interest to investigate the decay of 271 Ds in more detail.

  3/2+ [622]. The α decay populates the correspondig level in 271 Hs, which is isomeric in that isotope. It
  decays partly by α emission into the correponding level in 267 Sg, which decays by internal transition into
  the isomeric 1/2+ [620] state, that undergoes spontaneous ﬁssion. A second decay mode of the 3/2+ [622]
  isomeric state in 271 Hs is decay by internal transitions (probably in several steps) into the 11/2− [725]
  ground state, which then decays by α emission into the corresponding state in 267 Sg, followed by an
  internal transition into the ground state, which then undergoes α decay.

      273
          Ds
  The isotope 273 Ds was ﬁrst observed unambiguously as α-decay daughter of 277 Cn in bombardments of
  208
      Pb with 70 Zn ions at SHIP, GSI [102, 103]. Additional data using the same production method were
  obtained by K. Morita et al. [104] and by T. Sumita et al. [124].The data are presented in table 4.
  Summarizing the results of these experiments one obtains an α - decay energy Eα = 11.14 ±0.05 MeV
  and a half-life T1/2 = 0.19+0.14
                             −0.06 ms.
  In a recent experiment, performed at FLNR - JINR, Dubna Yu. Ts. Oganessian et al. [119] observed in
  a bombardment of 238 U with 40 Ar - projectiles two decay chains which they attributed to the decay of
  273
      Ds (Chains Ch6 and Ch7 in table 4).
  These chains, however, are not free of ambiguities. The α energies attributed to 265 Sg are signiﬁcantly
  lower than the literature values of Eα = 8.840 MeV (265 Sg(1)) and Eα = 8.690 MeV (265 Sg(2)) (see sect.
  5.9). Also the decay time of 273 Ds (41.703 m) in chain 7 is a factor ≈290 longer than the theoretical α
  half-life Tα = 0.145 ms [92].
  Nevertheless Yu.Ts. Oganessian et al. assign chain 6 to an isomer with the conﬁguration 1/2+ [620], set-
  tled at an excitation energy of E∗ ≈ 40 keV decaying into the corresponding level in 269 Hs (this isomer

         5.13 Isomers in odd-mass isotopes of even-Z elements Z >110

            289
                Fl
        Data on 289 Fl are scarce. First claim on the synthesis of this isotope came from an irradiation of 244 Pu
        with 48 Ca projectiles performed at the DGFRS, FLNR-JINR, Dubn by Yu.Ts. Oganessian et al. [125],
        who observed one decay chain and assigned a decay energy Eα = 9.71 MeV and a decay time (time
        diﬀerence between the implantation of the ER and the α decay) ∆t = 30.4 s to this isotope. This decay
        chain, however, never could be reproduced nor conﬁrmed.
        A couple of years later Yu.Ts. Oganessian et. al. [126] on the basis of eight observed decay chains
        reported diﬀerent decay data for this isotope: Eα = 9.82±0.06 MeV, T1/2 = 2.7+1.4    −0.7 s (see also table 5).
        Few years later, in an irradiation of 244 Pu with 48 Ca at the gas-ﬁlled separator TASCA at GSI, Darmstadt,
        three decay chains attributed to the decay of 289 Fl were obsered. The results are given in table 5. In two
        further experiments [129, 130] 289 Fl was produced by α decays of 293 Lv, synthesized in bombardments
        of 248 Cm with 48 Ca. In the SHIP experiment at GSI [129] one decay chain of 293 Lv was observed‖ In
        the GARIS experiment at RIKEN two decay chains were reported. The results are given in table 5.
            Evidently there are signiﬁkant diﬀerences in the energies and half-lives between the diﬀerent publi-
        cations, which, however, cannot necessarily be regarded as striking diﬀerences, as at this low number
        of registered events large ﬂuctuations the half-lives may occur, while energy diﬀerences may be due to
        calibration purposes.
        In a recent experiment at the gas-ﬁlled separator TASCA at GSI, 289 Fl was again procuced by the reac-
        tion 244 Pu(48 Ca,3n)289 Fl [132]. In this experiment 15 decay chains (in three chains, however, the α decay
        of 289 Fl were missing) were attributed to start from 289 Fl [132]. The energies were essentially distributed
        in the energy range E = (9.79 - 9.91) MeV, with two cases of signiﬁcant deviations, E(1) = 9.56±0.01
        MeV and E(2) = 9.99±0.02 MeV, with the latter energy reconstructed from an energy loss signal in the
        implantation detector and the residual energy measured with the ’box detector’. The diﬀerence of the
        latter energy value from the ’mean’ value Eα = 9.80±0.2 MeV. (In [132] the Qα (value), Qα = 9.94±0.02,
        is given.) was regarded as striking and ascribed to the decay of a diﬀerent state, as also the α energy of

     ‖ In [129] two decay chains of 293 Lv were reported. One of them, however, later was assigned to 291 Lv [131].
                                                                                                                         31


      the daughter nuclues 285 Cn was E = 9.30±0.02 MeV thus higher than the ’bulk’ of the decay energies,
      which were in the range E = (9.09 - 9.18) MeV. (Also a value reconstructed from an energy loss signal
      in the implantation detector and the residual energy measured in the ’box detector’.) So it was not
      incorporated into the main decay branch of 289 Fl.
      On the other side in [132] four decays of ’higher’ energies from the previous studies [128] due the similar
      half-life (see table 5) disregarding probable discrepancies in calibration, were regarded as possible decays
      of the isomeric state located at E∗ ≈ 70 keV. Due to the signiﬁcant diﬀerence in the energy, however, we
      will present here the decay properties as given in [132] (see also ﬁg. 3b there):
      289g
           Fl: Eα = 9.80±0.02 MeV, T1/2 = 2.5+0.8 −0.5 s,
      289m
           Fl: Eα = 9.99±0.02 MeV, T1/2 = 1.1+1.1 −0.4 s.
      As possible spins and parities Iπ = 15/2− for the ground state and Iπ = 3/2+ or Iπ = 1/2+ were consid-
      ered. However, with respect to possible problems with calibration, the assumption of an isomeric state
      in 289 Fl seems presently rather tentative.



      6. Isomers in odd-mass odd-Z nuclei



       6.1 Isomeric states in odd-mass mendelevium isotopes

          245
             Md
      The isotope 245 Md was ﬁrst synthesized by V. Ninov et al. [133] in the reaction 209 Bi(40 Ar,4n)245 Md
      at SHIP, GSI. Two activities were observed: a) a spontaneous ﬁssion activity of T1/2 = 0.90±0.025 ms,
      and b) an α activity of Eα1 = 8680±20 keV, Eα2 = 8620±20 keV, and a half-life of T1/2 = 0.35+0.23     −0.16
      s. Based on calculations of J. Randrup et al. [48], which predicted lower ﬁssion barriers for low-spin
      states in odd-mass nuclei and on calculations of low lying Nilsson levels in mendelevium isotopes by S.
      Cwiok et al. [95], the spontaneous ﬁssion activity was assigned to the ground state with the conﬁguration
      1/2− [521], and the α activity to an isomeric state with the conﬁguration 7/2− [514].
      The results of [133] were conﬁrmed in an experiment at TASCA, GSI by J. Khuyagbaatar et al. [134],
      who produced it in the reaction 197 Au(50 Ti,2n)245 Md. On the basis of the existing data conﬁguration
      assigment of [133] are still valid. Since, however, detailed decay studies of heavier odd-mass mendelevium
      isotopes resulted in the Nilsson level 7/2− [514] as the ground state [135], it seems meaningful also to
      assign the latter to the ground state of 245 Md and the 1/2− [521] level to the isomer.

          247
              Md
      The isotope 247 Md was ﬁrst observed in an irradiation of 209 Bi with 40 Ar at SHIP, GSI. On the basis
      of ﬁve decay events an α energy of Eα = 8424±25 keV and a half-life T1/2 = 2.9+1.7     −1.2 s were reported
      [136]. More detailed decay data were obtained in later studies, including γ spectroscopic investigations,
      also performed at SHIP [135, 137, 138, 139]. Besides the activity reported in [136], which was assigned
      to the 7/2− [514] ground state (Eα = 8421±1 keV + some weaker lines, T1/2 = 1.20±0.12 s∗∗ , bsf =
      0.0086±0.0010), on the basis of α decay and α - γ coincidence measurements a second activity with
      a half-life T1/2 = 0.23±0.3 s and an SF branch bsf = 0.20±0.02 was observed. It was attributed to
      an isomeric state with the conﬁguration 1/2− [521]. Besides the main α transitions, a rather broad line
      centered at Eα = 8720 keV, a weak α - γ - coincidence (Eα = 8451±11 keV, Eγ = 342.1±1.1 keV) was
      observed and interpreted as the decay 247m Md α  →
                                                          243
                                                              Es∗ γ→ 243 Es(gs). On the basis of the decay energy
                                       247g
      balance for the ground state (        Md) and the isomeric state (247m Md) the excitation energy of the
      isomer was estimated as E∗ = 153 keV [139].
      The excitation energy of the isomer was modiﬁed in a recent study of S.Y. Xu et al. [140]. Using dig-
      ital electronics on the basis of delayed coincidences between (Eα =8416 keV, Eγ =209 keV) and CE the
      7/2+ [633] level in the daughter 243 Es populated by the Eγ =209 keV transition and located at E∗ = 10
      keV in [139] was identiﬁed as an isomeric state of T1/2 = 5.0±0.5 µs and settled at E∗ = 85±16 keV.
      As the decay pattern of the isomeric state remained unchanged, the excitation energy of 247m Md was
      reduced to E∗ = 92 keV.

          249
              Md
      In analogy to the case of 247 Md also an 1/2− [521] isomeric state could be expected in 249 Md. An
      indication of a low lying 1/2− [521] state in 249 Md comes from the fact, that both states in 253 Lr,
      tentatively assigned as 1/2− [521] (253 Lr(1)) and 7/2− [514] (253 Lr(2)) decay by unhindered α transitions,
      meaning that the 1/2− [521] state should be populated by α decay of 253 Lr(1) [147]. It was also realized
      that α decays of 249 Md in the energy range Eα = (8050 - 8100) following α decay of 253 Lr(1) had a
      half-life T1/2 = 1.5+1.2
                          −0.5 s, while those, which followed decays of
                                                                          253
                                                                              Lr(2) had a half-life T1/2 = 53+27
                                                                                                             −13 s.

  ∗∗ A reanalysis of the data published in [136] showed that the long half-life published there was due to one event with an

accidentially long correlation time ∆t(ER-α).
32


     This was discussed as possibly α decay of an 1/2− [521] isomeric state. (The α decays in that energy
     range following decays of 253 Lr(2) can be explained due to energy summing of α particles and CE from
                                                                  γ(200.4keV ) 245
     the decay process 249 Md (7/2− [514]) α→
                                               245
                                                   Es (7/2− [514] →                Es (9/2+ ) CE
                                                                                              →
                                                                                                 245
                                                                                                     Es (7/2+ [633]
     [135]). This ﬁnding, however, was not reproduced in a later study [164], so it had to be left open if
     indeed a 1/2− [521] isomeric state was existing in 249 Md. Strictly spoken, the α line indicated in earlier
     experiments was conﬁrmed, but their half-life was in agreement with the half-life of the α decays in the
     ’main line’ and in addition they were correlated to α decays from both longlived states in 253 Lr.
     New information on the existence of an isomeric state in 249 Md came from a recent study of P. Brionnet et
     al. [141] who observed a weak α activity at Eα = 8350 keV with a half-life of T1/2 = 1.1±0.5 s, which they
     attributed to a 1/2− [521] isomer. It was interpreted as the decay 249 Md(1/2− [521] ) → 245 Es(3/2− [521]
     ), the latter one assumed as the ground state. Based on the energy balance for the decay of the ground
     state and isomeric state and the assumption that the 7/2+ [633] level in 245 Es populated by α − γ decay
     of 249g Md is placed not higher than 30 keV above the ground state, the excitation energy was settled at
     E∗ ≈(48-78) keV.
     The interpretation of the isomeric decay to a transition 1/2− [521]↓ → 3/2− [521]↑, however is ambiguous,
     as it requires a spin ﬂip. Such transitions are strongly hindered, which is in disagreement with the
     relatively low hindrance factor of HF = 28 given by the authors.

     6.2 Isomeric states in odd-mass lawrencium isotopes

        251
             Lr
     Information about decay of 251 Lr is scarce. First identiﬁcation of the isotope was reported by A. Leppänen
     [142], who observed a spontanous ﬁssion event following an α decay attributed to 255 Db. The time dif-
     ference between the α decay attributed to 255 Db and the ﬁssion event was ∆t(α-sf) = 39 ms, resulting in
     a half-life of T1/2 = 27+113
                              −13 ms [142].
     Some detailed investigation of 251 Lr was performed by T. Huang et al. [144]. They observed two α - lines
     of Eα1 = 9246 ± 19 keV and Eα2 = 9210 ± 19 keV with slightly diﬀerent half-lives of T1/2 (α1) = 24.4+7.0 −4.5
     ms and T1/2 (α2) = 42+42−14 ms. Theoretical half-lives according to [92] are Tcalc = 18 ms for Eα1 and Tcalc
     = 23 ms for Eα2 . So both transitions have to be considered as unhindered. The energy of the daughter α
     decays were Eα = 8430 ± 13 keV, the half-lives were T1/2 = 1.20+0.52−0.28 s for the daughter decays following
     Eα1 and T1/2 = 0.72+0.98
                           −0.26 s for the daughter decays following Eα2 . These decay properties are in-line with
     the known decay properties of 247g Md, but signiﬁcantly diﬀerent from that of the isomeric state 247m Md
     (most intense α line: Eα = 8720 keV, T1/2 = 0.23±0.03 s). Nevertheless T. Huang et al. attributed
     Eα1 to the ground state to ground state decay of 251 Lr (251g Lr (7/2− [514]) → 247g Md (7/2− [514])) and
     Eα2 to the decay of an isomer 251m Lr located at E∗ = 117±27 keV into the isomeric state 247m Md
     (251m Lr (1/2− [521]) → 247m Md (1/2− [521])). This interpretation, however, would require that 247m Md
     decays predominantly by internal transition into the ground state 247g Md. Although this transition
     was not observed so far in the direct investigation of 247 Md [139], it cannot be ruled out as the half-
     life for an E3 transition (1/2− → 7/2− ) is expected to be ≈0.2 s according to the Weisskopf estimate [14].

        253
            Lr
     The isotope 253 Lr was ﬁrst identiﬁed as the α - decay daughter of 257 Db. Two α lines (Eα1 = 8800 ± 20
     keV (irel ≈ 0.56), Eα2 = 8722 ± 20 keV (irel ≈0.44)) and a half-life of T1/2 = 1.3+0.6
                                                                                        −0.3 s were attributed to
     this isotope [146]. In a more detailed study [147] the results were essentially conﬁrmed, it was, however,
     found, that the α decays stem from diﬀerent levels in 253 Lr, populated by α decay of diﬀerent levels in
     257
         Db. The assignment of the lines to decay of the ground state and the isomeric state turned out to be
     rather diﬃcult. The decay scenario of both isotopes, 257 Db and 253 Lr is discussed in detail in [147].
     Further studies of the decay of 257 Db performed at SHIP [143, 164] did not deliver new insight into
     the decay, level ordering and excitation energy of the isomeric state in 253 Lr, which is presently still
     uncertain. The results were recently conﬁrmed by T. Huang et al. [144]. They identiﬁed another α
     line (8660±20 keV), which was attributed to the decay of the ground state of 253 Lr into an 11/2− state
     in 249 Md located at E∗ = (127±24) keV. The half-life of 253 Lr(2) was given as T1/2 = 2.46±0.32 s,
     considerably longer than the value T1/2 = 1.32±0.14 s, given in [164]. A reanalysis of the data, however,
     showed that the value given in [164] underestimated the half-life and resulted in an improved value of
     T1/2 = 2.23+0.22
                  −0.18 s. The suggested decay schemes from the SHIP experiments and [144] are compared in
     ﬁg.24.
     In a recent study by P. Brionnet et al. [141] the earlier results and conclusions were conﬁrmed. In
     addition the authors settle the isomeric state at E∗ ≈(6-24) keV.

        255
            Lr
     The isotope 255 Lr was probably ﬁrst observed by V.A. Druin [148], who reported the observation of an α
     emitter (Eα = 8.38 MeV, T1/2 ≈ 20 s) in bombardments of 243 Am with 16 O. The ﬁrst deﬁnite assignment
     of an α emitter (Eα = 8370±18 keV, T1/2 22±5 s) to 255 Lr was done by K. Eskola [149]. The data were
     later conﬁrmed by C.E. Bemis et al. [150], who in addition observed an α line of Eα = 8410±18 keV,

     with a relative intensity of irel = 0.4. and gave a half-life of T1/2 = 21.5±5.0 s.
     Indication for the existence of a low lying isomeric state in 255 Lr came from experiments performed at
     the University of Jyväskylä, Jyväskylä, (Finland), later continued at GANIL, Caen (France) [151] and at
     SHIP, GSI [152] where an additional α line with a shorter half-life (Eα = 8457±2 keV, T1/2 = 2.53±0.13
     s [151] or Eα = 8470±10 keV, T1/2 = 2.1±0.1 s [152]) were observed. While the previously known tran-
     sitions were attributed to the decay of the 1/2− [521] Nilsson level, the new activity was attributed to the
     decay of the 7/2− [514] Nilsson level in both studies. However, while F.P. Heßberger et al. attributed the
     1/2− [521] level to the isomeric state and the 7/2− [514] level to the ground state, in analogy to 253 Lr, A.
     Chatillon et al. attributed on the basis of more detailed data the 1/2− [521] level to the ground state and
     the 7/2− [514] level to the isomeric state, which they located at E∗ = 37 keV. The latter interpretation
     was corroborated by further studies at SHIP [153]. While in the α decay spectrum the Eα = 8373 keV
     - line (energy value from [153]) appeared to be much stronger than the Eα = 8467 keV - line (energy
     value from [153]), in correlation with decays of the high spin K isomer in 255 Lr [153, 154] the Eα = 8467
     keV - line appeared to be stronger. This feature was interpreted in the following way: the decay of the
     K isomer preferably populates the state of the higher spin (7/2− [514]), which then decays either by α
     emission (8467 keV) or by internal transition (IT) into the 1/2− [521] ground state, then decaying by
     emission of Eα = 8373 keV α particles.
     The excitation energy of the isomer was measured directly using SHIPTRAP. A value of E∗ = 32±2 keV
     was obtained [47].
     A decay scheme is shown in ﬁg.25


        257
             Lr
     First information on decay properties of 257 Lr came from experiments on direct production of this iso-
     tope by K. Eskola et al. [149] and from an experiment on synthesis of 261,262 Db (at this time named
     261,262
             Ha) by A. Ghiorso et al. [155], where 257 Lr was produced by α decay of 261 Db. While K. Eskola
     et al. reported two α enegies (8.87±0.02 MeV, 8.81±0.02 MeV) and a half-life T1/2 = 0.6±0.1 s, A.
     Ghiorso et al. mentioned only the Eα = 8.87 MeV transition.
     More detailed information on the decay properties was obtained in experiments performed at SHIP, GSI
     where 257 Lr was produced either by α decay of 261 Db [64] or by EC decay of 257m,257g Rf [64, 77]. The
     results of the analysis are shown in ﬁg. 26a,b,c. Fig. 26a shows the energy distribution of the 257 Lr α de-
     cays following α decays of 261 Db, ﬁgs. 26b and 26c show the energy distribution of 257 Lr when produced
     by EC decay of 257 Rf. While ﬁg. 26b shows the energy distribution of 257 Lr at ’indirect’ production
     of 257 Rf, i.e. by α decay of 261 Sg, where only the ground state 257g Rf is populated, ﬁg. 26c shows the
     energy distribution of 257 Lr at ’direct’ production of 257 Rf via the reaction 208 Pb(50 Ti,n)257 Rf, where
     257m
          Rf and 257g Rf, are produced at a ratio ≈2:1. From comparison of the spectra it is evident that the
     occurrence of the ’low energy’ line (Eα = 8811 keV) is essentilally related to the production of 257g Rf, the
     occurrence of the ’high energy’ line with the production of 257m Rf and production by α decay of 261 Db.
     So it seems straightforward to assign the α lines to decays from diﬀerent levels in 257 Lr. This interpretion
     is supported by the half-life analysis, For the Eα = 8811 keV - line a value of T1/2 = 0.20+0.16  −0.06 s was
     obtained, for the Eα = 8878 keV - line a value of T1/2 = 1.24+0.85
                                                                    −0.36 s. As the groud-state of
                                                                                                    257
                                                                                                        Rf is a low
     spin state (Nilsson conﬁguration 1/2+ [620]), while the isomer is a high spin state (Nilsson conﬁguration
     11/2− [725], see sect. 5.6) and the ground state of 261 Db is predicted as 9/2+ [624], the level related to
     emission of the Eα = 8811 keV alpha particles is related to a low spin state, tentalively assigned as an
     1/2− [521] isomeric state, which is predicted as low lying level in lawrencium isotopes [94].
     As the ground state of 261 Db is predicted as 9/2+ [624] and the α decay of this isotope is only slightly
     hindered (HF ≈3, using a theoretical α half-life Tα = 0.37 s [92]) the α line connects transitions of the
     same Nilsson conﬁguration. So the longer-lived level in 257 Lr may also assigned as 9/2+ [624]. It should
     be noticed, however, that the 7/2− [514] Nilsson - state is predicted as the ground state in 257 Lr and as
     a low lying level in 261 Db [94] and, considering the uncertainty of theoreical predictions, the 7/2− [514]
     Nilsson - state could be ground state in both isotopes and the Eα = 8878 keV - line could represent a
     transition between these two states. As, however, this state is seemingly populated by EC decay of the
     11/2− [725] level in 257 Rf and since the EC decay 11/2− → 9/2+ is ’single forbidden’, while the EC decay
     11/2− → 7/2− is ’double forbidden’ [157], the 9/2+ [624] Nilsson level seems to be the better choice.
     Decay patterns and level schemes are shown in ﬁg.27.

     ∆t(ER-SF(1)) = 99 ms and ∆t(ER-SF(2)) = 4 ms. Speciﬁcally the value ∆t(ER-SF(2)), much shorter

than ∆t(ER-SF(1)) and ∆t(ER-α) suggests the existence of two ’longlived’ (T1/2 > 1 ms) states in
255
   Db. New decay data were reported by J.L. Pore et al. [158] who attributed 55 SF events and three α
decays (9245±47, 9396±47, 9232±47 keV) with a half-life T1/2 = 2.6+0.4
                                                                   −0.3 ms to . A longlived component
was not observed. Thus the two longlived events from [142] seem questionable and therefore presently
there is no real evidence for a low lying isomeric
state in 255 Db.




      257
       Db
The existence of an isomeric state in257 Db was not straight forward on the basis of the level scheme
predicted at the time of publication of [147], suggessting a level ordering 9/2+ [624] (gs), 7/2− [514] (230
keV), 5/2− [512] (290 keV), 1/2− [521] (460 keV) [95] (see ﬁg. 28a). The same level ordering, but some-
what diﬀerent energies were later predicted by A. Parkhomenko and A. Sobiczewski [94] (see ﬁg. 28b). In
this scenario the low spin state 1/2− could decay via 1/2− [521] E2         −      M1      −       E1
                                                                     → 5/2 [512] → 7/2 [514] → 9/2 [624]
                                                                                                         +
                                                          −                                              −
(ground state). Therefore tentatively in [147] the 1/2 [521] level was thus placed below the 7/2 [514]
and 5/2− [512] levels and the decay scheme shown in ﬁg. 29a) was suggested. In a further study at
SHIP [143, 164] where a larger amount of decays was collected it was realized that the lines at Eα =
9066 keV (FWHM = 64 keV) and Eα = 8965 keV (FWHM = 53 keV) were broader than the line at
Eα = 9155 keV (FWHM = 28 keV) [160]. It was assumed that these lines were strongly inﬂuenced by
energy summing with CE, speciﬁcally the line at Eα = 9066 keV now rather interpreted as the energy
sum of the decay populating the 9/2+ [624] level and CE from the internal transition into the 7/2− [514]
ground state of 253 Lr than the decay 257 Db (9/2+ [624]) α →
                                                              253
                                                                  Lr (7/2− [514]) as such a transition requires
a change of parity and in addition a spin ﬂip, and is thus strongly hindered (HF >> 1000 [115]. The
assumption of a direct decay 9/2+ [624]) IC        −
                                            → 7/2 [514] was in contradiction with the experimental data,
as such a transition has E1 - multipolarity, is only little converted, thus does not lead to broadening of
the α lines, while on the other hand only one γ event (Eγ = 102.2 keV was registered in coincidence
with an α particle (Eα = 8941 keV), whereas ten events were expected for an E1 transition. It thus
was speculated, that the 9/2+ [624] level might decay by a low energy E1 transition into the 11/2− of
the rotational band built up on the 7/2− [514] ground state which then deexcites essentially by two M1
transitions into the ground state as sketched in ﬁg. 28b.
A diﬀerent level ordering was predicted by G.G. Adamian et al. [161] (see ﬁg. 28c). They predict the
1/2− [521] as the ground state, the 5/2− [512] (E∗ ≈8 keV) and 9/2+ [624] (E∗ ≈17 keV) levels as long-lived
isomeric states decaying at least partly by α emission.
Further results on the decay of 257m,257g Db were published by P. Brionnet et al. [141]. Essentially the
results of [147, 164] were conﬁrmed, in addition three γ events at Eγ = 145 keV in coincidence with
Eα = (8.90-8.95) MeV were observed. On the basis of Geant4 simulations they concluded that the spec-
ulation in [164] of a decay pattern 9/2+ [624] E1→ 11/2
                                                         − M1
                                                            → 9/2
                                                                    − M1       −
                                                                       → 7/2 [514] is unlikely. To solve the
                                                 257
’problem’ they suggested the ground state of         Db rather being the Nilsson - level 9/2− [505] decaying
essentially into the corresponding level in 253 Lr and with smaller intensities into the 11/2− state of the
rotational band built up on it and the 11/2− level built up on the ground state of 253 Lr. The weak point
of such an assignment is the fact that none of the theoretical calculations predict the 9/2− [505] Nilsson
level in odd-A odd-Z isotopes in elements Z<107 at excitation energies E∗ <1 MeV.
To explain the low energy of the 9/2− [505] level, the authors speculate on an inﬂuence of higher order
deformations, speciﬁcally hexadecapole deformation (denoted ν4 or β4 ). Calculations of R.R. Chasman
et al. [162] predict a strong increase of the energy of the 9/2+ [624] and 1/2− [521] levels when ν4 changes
from ν4 = 0.04 to ν4 = -0.04, while the energy of the 9/2− [505] level slightly decreases. While at ν4
= -0.04 one obtains ∆E(9/2− ,9/2+ ) = 0.87 MeV, at ν4 = +0.04 one obtains ∆E(9/2− ,9/2+ ) = 2.08
MeV. The change of β4 in dubnium isotopes is reproduced by the calculations. Cwiok et al. [95] obtain
a change from β4 = 0.003 (255 Db) to β4 = -0.035 (263 Db), A. Prakhomenko and A. Sobiczewski [94]
obtain a change from β4 = 0.008 (253 Db) to β4 = -0.036 (263 Db), but still in both calculations for none
of the isotopes the 9/2− [505] level is located below 1 MeV. From this side the assignment of this level
to the ground state of 257 Db is quite uncertain. More detailed investigations are necessary to clarify the
situation.
The decay scheme presented by P. Brionnet et al. [141] is shown in ﬁg. 29c. They settle the isomer
at E∗ ≈(59-89) keV and postulate besides the strong α transition 257 Db(9/2− [505]) → 253 Lr(9/2− [505])
two weaker ones 257 Db(9/2− [505]) → 253 Lr(11/2− ) (member of the rotational band built up on the
9/2− [505] level) and 257 Db(9/2− [505]) → 253 Lr(9/2− ) (member of the rotational band built up on the
7/2− [514] ground state). At least the latter transition is unlikely, as it requires a spin-ﬂip, thus should be
highly hindered and thus should not be oberserved on the basis of the number of the total decays of 257 Db.

     7. Isomers in odd-odd nuclei



     7.1 Isomeric states in odd-odd mendelevium isotopes

         246
             Md
     The isotope 246 Md was ﬁrst identiﬁed at SHIP, GSI in bombardments of 209 Bi targets with 40 Ar pro-
     jectiles by V. Ninov et al. [133]. Two groups of α decays (Eα = (8500-8560) keV, 8740±20 keV) and a
     half-life T1/2 = 1.0±0.4 s were attributed to this isotope.
     Later, more intense studies by S. Antalic et al. [138] showed that besides the known activity, now denoted
     as 246m1 Md another activity (246m2 Md) had to be assigned. Reported [138] decay properties are:
     a) 246m1 Md: (Eα = (8380-8640) keV, 8744±10 keV, T1/2 = 0.9±0.2 s.
     b) 246m2 Md: Eα = 8178±10 keV, T1/2 = 4.4±0.8 s.
     It was also found, that 246m1 Md decays to a high extent by EC (bEC >0.77) accompanied by a high
     EC-delayed ﬁssion branch (bECDF >0.1).
     No spin and parity assignments for 246m1 Md and 246m2 Md, also no (tentative) assignment of both states
     to the ground state and the isomeric state were done. Taking the α energy of Eα = 8740±10 keV (as-
     suming ground state to ground state transition) for calculating the decay Q-value 246 Md and the sum of
     the α decay energy (Eα = 8178±10 keV) and the energies of the coincident gammas (Eγ = 252, 279 keV)
     (see ﬁg.6 in [138]) for calculating the decay Q-value of 246m2 Md one obtains values of Q(246m1 Md) ≈ 8890
     keV and Q(246m2 Md) ≈ 8840 keV, which would suggest to attribute Q( 246m1 Md) to the ground state.
     Deﬁnitely it is not known presently if these Q-values indeed represent the Q-value diﬀerence between
     246m1
            Md or 246m2 Md and the ground state of 242 Es. So it is presently uncertain which of the activity is
     the ground state and which is the isomer.
     In the neighbouring odd-even nucleus 245 Fm low lying levels 7/2− [743] and 1/2+ [631] [33, 35] are sup-
     posed or predicted for the the N=145th neutron, while low lying proton levels in the neighbouring Z = 101
     nucleus 247 Md are 7/2− [514] and 1/2+ [521]. So the odd nucleon can couple to states Iπ = 1− , 4− , 4+ ,
     and 7+ . The spin diﬀerences ∆I(1− , 4− or 4+ ) = 3, ∆I( 7+ , 4− or 4+ ) = 3, and ∆I( 1− , 7+ ) = 6, suggest
     indeed the existence of isomeric states, but presently it is not possible to assign safely spin and parity
     to 246m1 Md or 246m2 Md. It should, however, be remarked that the large EC-delayed ﬁssion branch of
     246m2
            Md indicates that EC decay of this state populates notably high spin states in 246 Fm, which can
     be regarded as an indication that 246m2 Md is a high spin state.

         250
           Md
     The isotope 250 Md was ﬁrst reported by P. Eskola [163] who produced it in irradiations 15 N + 240 Pu,
     16
        O + 241 Am 12,13 C + 243 Am. She reported two α lines, Eα = 7.75±0.02, 7.82±0.02 MeV and a half-life

                   - n 7/2+[624]              + n 7/2+[624]       +n 7/2+[624]        -n   7/2+[624]




Fig. 31: a) Predicted low lying levels of 250 Md([165], b) experimentally (tentatively) proposed decay schemes
of 250m,250g Md [166].




              T1/2 = 52 ± 6 s. The isotope was found to desintegrate predominantly by β + , EC - decay. The data
              were later conﬁrmed by F.P. Heßberger et al. [146] and by S. Antalic et al. [153] by studying α decay of
              254
                  Lr or 258 Db ( 209 Bi(50 Ti,n)258 Db) → 254 Lr → 250 Md or 209 Bi(48 Ca,3n)254 Lr → 250 Md). The discovery
              of isomeric states in 254 Lr (see sect. 7.2) and 258 Db (see sect. 7.3) suggested a possible existence of an
              isomeric state in 250 Md also decaying partly by α emission and initiated a careful (re-)analysis of data
              accumulated in a detailed decay study of 258 Db [166] and measured at SHIP in the previous studies
              [146, 153, 164]).
              The analysis resulted in the identiﬁcation of two α peaks (Eα = 7741 ± 24, 7781 ± 21 keV) and a broad
              distribution of α decays at Eα > 7.8 MeV. Both groups were found to have slightly diﬀerent half-lives.
              For events Eα < 7.8 MeV a value T1/2 = 42.5 ± 4.5 s was obtained, for events Eα > 7.8 MeV a value
              T1/2 = 59.5 ± 9.5 s was obtained.
              A couple of γ events was found in coincidence with α decays. The results are listed in table 6. The
              energy sums Eα + Eγ are
              a) E = 8152 keV (7741+411)
              b) E = 8160 keV (7781+379)
              c) E = 8027 keV (7875 + 152)
              The similar values in a) and b) suggest that the decays stem from the same level in 250 Md, the higher
              values in a) and b) than in c) suggest that these decays stem from a level having a higher excitation
              energy than the one in c), which thus tentatively is assigned to the decay of the ground state. The
              assignment of a) and b) to an excited level in 250 Md is corroborated by the systematics of α - decay
              Q-values of mendelevium isotopes as shown in ﬁg. 30. While the Q-value obtained from c) (Q = 8157
              keV) ﬁts quite well into the systematics the one obtained from a) and b) (Q = 8282 keV) introduces
              a kink. The decay scheme of 250 Md derived from the experimental data is shown in ﬁg. 31b. As few
              events of Eγ = 118.6, 151.9 keV were observed in coincidence with αs E<7.8 MeV, we assume also some
              internal transitions from the levels populated by the Eα = 7741, 7781 keV to the level(s) emitting the E
              = 118.6, 151.9 keV γs as indicated in ﬁg. 31b.
              Low lying states, shown in ﬁg. 31a of 250 Md have been calculated by P.C. Sood et al. [165]. As seen the
              Iπ = 7− state placed at E∗ = 80±30 keV was predicted as an isomeric state decaying to large extent, by
              β + /EC and/or α emission, which is in quite good agreement with the identiﬁcation of the isomeric state
              at E∗ = 123 keV.

        254
            Md
     The isotope 254 Md was ﬁrst observed by P.R. Fields et al. [167] in irradiations of an einsteinium target
     (253 Es content 99.4 per cent) with 4 He projectiles. Two EC activities with half-lives T1/2 = 10±3 min
     and T1/2 = 28±8 min were attributed to this isotope. α decay was not reported.
     In a study at SHIP, GSI of K isomeric states in 254 No [168], the isotope 254 Md was produced as a by-
     product via EC - days of 254 No. It was shown by coincidences between fermium K X rays and γ - events
     (Eγ = 693.3, 648.3 keV) that EC - decay populates the Iπ = 2+ level at E∗ = 693.79 keV in 254 Fm, by
     strongly converted transitions from higher levels. The Iπ = 2+ level at E∗ = 693.79 keV in 254 Fm is
     known from β − decay studies of 254 Es [14]. No new information about the isomeric state 254m Md was
     obtained [55].

        258
            Md
     First identiﬁcation of an isomeric state was performed by D.C. Hoﬀman et al. [169, 170], who reported
     EC decay as the dominant decay mode and a half-life T1/2 = 43±3 min.
     More detailed decay properties were given by K.J. Moody et al. [171]. They established the main decay
     channel as EC/β + , with a branching bEC/β + ≥ 0.7, and upper limits for other decay channels (bα ≤
     0.012, bβ − ≤ 0.3, bSF ≤ 0.3). Spin and parity were tentatively assigned as Iπ = 1− , and a half-life T1/2
     = 57.0±0.9 min was reported. No value for the excitation energy was given so far.



     7.2 Isomeric states in odd-odd lawrencium isotopes

        252
            Lr
     The isotope 252 Lr was ﬁrst identiﬁed as α - decay product of 256 Db [147]; two α lines (Eα1 = 9018 ±
     20 keV (irel ≈ 0.75)) and (Eα2 = 8974 ± 20 keV (irel ≈ 0.25)) and a half-life T1/2 = 0.36+0.36−0.11 s were
     attributed to this isotope. Some more events were reported from a decay study of 260 Bh by S.L. Nelson et
     al. [145], among them two events (Eα = 9.61 MeV, Eα = 9.82 MeV) with signiﬁcantly diﬀerent energies.
     But both events were observed as ’sum’ events measuring the energy loss in the ’stop detector’ and the
     residual energy in the ’box detector’ surroundig the ’stop detector’, which could lead to signiﬁcantly
     diﬀerent energies due to calibration purposes. The event of the higher energy was followed by a 248 Md
     decay of Eα = 8.46 MeV, signiﬁcantly higher than the known α - decay energies of this isotope (Eα =
     8.32, 8.36 MeV). So it was speculated if it could be due to the decay of an isomeric state, but due to the
     low quality of the data, no deﬁnite conclusions were drawn. A recent decay study of 256 Db by J.L. Pore
     et al. [172], did not give a signature for the existence of an isomeric state in 252 Lr.

        254
            Lr
     The isotope 254 Lr was ﬁrst synthesized by α decay of 258 Db [146]. More detailed information on its
     decay properties was obtained from further decay studies of 258 Db [164, 166] and direct production in
     the reaction 209 Bi(48 Ca,3n)254 Lr [153]. The α spectrum turned out to be quite ’structureless’, indicating
     a strong inﬂuence of energy summing between α - particles and CE. Nevertheless some more detailed
     informations on the decay of 254 Lr were obtained from α - γ measurements [166]. Information on the
     existence of a low lying isomeric state in 254 Lr came from a detailed decay study of 258 Db [164, 166] which
     resulted in a notable population of a long lived excited state in 254 Lr by α decay of 258 Db. It was found
     on the basis of the energy balance, that the E = 9185 keV - transition (in the former publication [164]
     given as E = 9196 keV) of 258 Db, did not populate the ground state of 254 Lr. As this line was not found

Fig. 32: Predicted (a) ([173]) and (b) experimentally (tentatively) assigned [166] low lying levels of 254 Lr.



               in coincidence with γ rays or being inﬂuenced by energy summing with CE, it was concluded that this
               transition must populate a level in 254 Lr with a ’long’ half-life (>> 1 µs). Due to the above mentioned
               strong inﬂuence of energy summing between α particles and CE α decays of 254m Lr and 254g Lr could not
               be disentangled, it just could be shown, that the α decays of 254m Lr are essentially concentrated in the
               energy range Eα ≈ (8430-8520) keV and in addition a small line at Eα = 8395 keV was indicated, while
               the energies of α particles attributed to the decay of 254g Lr were concentrated in broader range Eα ≈
               (8350-8520) keV. Most of the γ events could be attributed to the decay of levels in 250 Md populated by
               α decay of 254g Lr. It should be emphasized, that population of the isomeric state in 250 Md at E∗ = 123
               keV by decay of 254g Lr could not be established so far. Also no detailed decay properties of 254m Lr were
               obtained so far.
               The half-lives of the ground state and the isomeric state were measured as T1/2 (254g Lr) = 11.9 ± 0.9 s
               and T1/2 (254m Lr) = 20.3 ± 4.2 s [166]. On the basis of the decay properties of 258g,258m Db the excitation
               energy of the isomer was determined as E∗ = 108 keV. Existence and excitation energy of the isomeric
               state were conﬁrmed by direct mass measurents of 254g,254m Lr, which resulted in E∗ = 107 ± 4 keV for
               the isomer [47].
               Low energy levels (E∗ < 250 keV) in 254 Lr have been predicted by R. Gowrishankar et al. on the basis of a
               ’Two-Quasi-Particle-Rotor-Model’ [173]. The results are shown in ﬁg. 32. The ground state is predicted
               as Kπ = 1+ and an isomeric state Kπ = 4+ is predicted at E∗ ≈75 keV, which is in quite fair agreement
               with the experimental value of E∗ = 108 keV. Tentative spin and parity assigments are, however, diﬀer-
               ent. Based on the experimental results the ground state was assigned as Kπ = 4+ , the isomeric state as
               Kπ = 1− (see ﬁg. 35). This assignment was based on the assumed ground state conﬁguration Kπ = 0−
               of 258 Db and the low α-decay hindrance factor HF≈30 for the transition 258g Db → 254m Lr which rather
               favors Kπ = 1− than Kπ = 4+ as the latter conﬁguration would require an angular momentum change
               ∆K = 3 and a change of the parity which requires a much larger hindrance factor (see sect. 7.1).
               Here, however, two items should be considered:
               a) the spin-parity assigmnent of 258 Db is only tentative,
               b) the calculations are based on the energies of low lying levels in the neighboring odd mass nuclei, in the
               respecting case 253 No (N = 151) and 253 Lr (Z = 103). The lowest Nilsson levels in 253 No are 9/2− [734]
               for the ground state and 5/2+ [622] for a shortlived isomer at E∗ = 167 keV [64]. In 253 Lr tentative as-
               signments of the ground state (7/2− [514]) and 1/2− [521] for a low lying isomer are given in [147]. The
               energy of the isomer is experimentally not well established, for the calculations a value of 30 keV was
               taken [173], while recently a value E+ ≈ (6-24) keV was reported by P. Brionnet et al. [141]. It should
               be noted, however, that for the neighboring N = 152 isotope of lawrencium, 255 Lr the ground state had
               been determined as the Nilsson level 1/2− [521], while 7/2− was attributed to a low lying isomeric state
42


     at E∗ = 37 keV [151]. Therefore, with respect to the uncertain starting conditions, the results of the
     calculations although not in ’perfect agreement’ with the experimental results, are still promising, and
     may be improved in future.




     7.3 Isomeric states in odd-odd dubnium isotopes


           258
            Db
     258
         Db is an illustrative example for the diﬃculties identifying an isomeric state in case when the decay
     properties of the isomer and the ground state are similar. Its identiﬁcation thus will be discussed a bit
     more exhaustive.
     The isotope 258 Db was ﬁrst identiﬁed at SHIP in the reaction 209 Bi(50 Ti,n)258 Db [146]. It was found to
     have a quite ’complex’ α - spectrum, a half-life T1/2 = 4.4+0.9
                                                                   −0.6 s was measured. Also SF with a compara-
     ble half-life was observed. As spontaneous ﬁssion of odd-odd nuclei is strongly hindered the ﬁssion events
     were attributed to 258 Rf, a known spontaneously ﬁssioning isotope, produced by EC decay of 258 Db. This
     interpretation was later proven by measuring K X-rays of rutherfordium (stemming from the EC process
     or converted internal transitions from excited levels in 258 Rf populated by the EC process) and SF events
     [175].
     First indication of an isomeric state in 258 Db (and also in 254 Lr) was obtained in an experiment performed
     at SHIP in 1997 from comparision of the spectra of α events of 258 Db followed by α decays of 254 Lr or
     254
         No [164]. It was realized that the α line at Eα = 9166 keV was only present when the α decays of
     258
         Db were followed by α decays of 254 Lr, while the line at Eα = 9196 keV was seen when α decays of
     both isotopes 254 Lr and 254 No followed. This feature was conﬁrmed in a more detailed study [166] (see
     ﬁg. 33) and could be interpreted in two ways:
     a) the α line at Eα = 9196 keV followed the by decay of 254 No stems from α decay of 258 Rf produced
     by EC decay of 258 Db. The α line itself had to be assumed to accidentally overlay with an α transition
     energy of 258 Db. Also a low EC branch of 254 Lr and a so far not known high α branch of 258 Rf had to be
     assumed. The interpretation seemed tempting, but had to be rejected after α decay of 258 Rf had been
     identiﬁed and the decay energy was measured as Eα = 9.05 ± 0.03 MeV [86].
     b) the second interpretation requires isomeric states in 258 Db and also in 254 Lr. The α line at Eα =
     9196 keV and at Eα = 9166 keV stem from diﬀerent levels in 258 Db and also populate diﬀerent levels in
     254
         Lr with the level populated by the line at Eα = 9196 keV having a signiﬁcantly larger EC - branch.
     The level populated by the Eα = 9196 keV transition may be the excited one, decaying also notably by
     internal transition to the ground state.
     The assumption of two diﬀerent levels decaying by α emission was corroborated by the results from a
     decay study of 262 Bh, for which two levels decaying by α emission were known [174]. It was shown that
     the Eα = 9196 keV - line was practically only preceded by decays of 262 Bh(2) [164] (see also ﬁg. 38 in
     sect.7.4).
     As a result, a broad α energy distribution in the range Eα ≈ (9090-9353) keV and a half-life T1/2 =
     4.3±0.5 s were attributed to 258 Db(1), while an α energy Eα = 9196 ± 10 keV and a half-life T1/2 =
     1.9±0.5 s were attributed to 258 Db(2). It remained, however, unclear, which activity represented the
     ground state and which the isomer.
     The situation could be clariﬁed in a detailed study at SHIP, including also α - γ coincidence measure-
     ments [166]. An analysis of the half-lives of the broad energy distribution of the α decays, subdivided
     in 20 keV- or 40 keV - bins is shown in ﬁg. 34. Evidently two regions of lower half-lives are present,
     one at Eα ≈ 9070 keV and one at Eα ≈ 9180 keV. Halﬂives of T1/2 = 4.32 ± 0.42 s for 258 Db(1) (Eα
     < 9040 keV, (9090-9170) keV, >9210 keV) and T1/2 = 2.91 ± 0.24 s for 258 Db(2) (Eα (9065-9085) keV,
     (9175-9205) keV) are obtained. We have here to note, that there might still be an overlay of αs from
     both states due to limited detector resolution and probable energy summing between α - particles and
     CE that eﬀect the half-life measurement.
     The decay schemes for 258m Db and 258g Db and the partial level scheme of 254 Lr derived from the mea-
     sured data is shown in ﬁg. 35. Tentative spin and parity assignments of the ground state and the
     isomeric state were based on the EC decay properties of 258 Db [175] and the coupling rules of unpaired
     protons and neutrons in odd-odd nuclei as suggested by M.H. Brennan and A.M. Bernstein [176]. The
     latter resulted in Iπ = 0− , 5+ , 10− as low lying states [166]. From time distributions of ﬁssion events
     of 258 Rf produced by EC decay of 258 Db, somewhat diﬀerent half-lifes resulted for events preceded by
     CE (T1/2 = 4.4±1.0 s) or not preceded by CE (T1/2 = 3.6±0.3 s). The ﬁrst group was assigned to EC
     decays populating excited levels in 258 Rf, the second group was interpreted as a mixture of decays into
     the ground state of 258 Rf (Iπ = 0+ ) and decays into excited levels where the CE was not registered.
     Consequently spin/parity Iπ = 0− were attributed the state of the lower half-life, the ground state of
     258
         Db and the spins Iπ = 5+ or 10− to the isomeric state having the longer half-life.

                        262
                            Db
                    The observation of 262 Db was ﬁrst reported by A. Ghiorso et al. [155], who reported a complex α
                    spectrum with a ’main’ line at Eα = 8.45 MeV and indication of another line at Eα = 8.66 MeV, which,
                    however, was assumed to be overlayed by α decays of the daughter product 258 Lr. Fission events with
                    comparable half-lives were reported by J.R. Bemis et al. [177] and V.A. Druin et al. [178].
                    In further experments 262 Db was produced either directly by the reaction 248 Cm(19 F,5n)262 Db [183] or
                    by decay of 266 Bh [179, 180, 181, 182]. All these experiments suﬀered from a low number of observed
                    decays.
                    Experiments with higher ’statistics’ recently have been performed by H. Haba et al. who either produced
                    it in the reaction 248 Cm(19 F,5n)262 Db [184] or by α decay of 266 Bh [185]. The α spectra registered in
                    both experiments are shown in ﬁgs. 36a) and 36b). As α decay energy regions of 262 Db and 258 Lr overlap
                    in case of decay of 266 Bh only data from triple correlations 266 Bh α →
                                                                                               262
                                                                                                   Db α
                                                                                                      →
                                                                                                        258
                                                                                                            Lr α
                                                                                                               → are considered.
                    Evidently there are two groups of α decays, Eα1 = (8.35-8.55) MeV and Eα2 = (8.60-8.80) MeV. Half-lives
                    of both groups are T1/2 = 35.5+5.2                            +5.5
                                                     −4.2 s (Eα1 ) and T1/2 = 21.8−3.6 s (Eα2 ). The diﬀerence is already outside
                    the sum of the 1σ error bars, and therefore both groups of α decays have to be considered to stem from
                    the decay of diﬀerent levels. This assumption is corroborated the analysis of α - α correlations 266 Bh α  →
                    262
                        Db α→ (see section 7.4 for a detailed discussion).



                    7.4 Isomeric states in odd-odd bohrium isotopes

                         262
                           Bh
                    The isotope 262 Bh was ﬁrst synthesized at SHIP, GSI, by G. Münzenberg et al. [186], which was regarded
                    as the discovery of element 107. At this time ﬁve decays with a mean energy Eα = 10376±35 keV and
                    a half-life T1/2 = 4.7+2.3
                                          −1.6 ms and one decay with an energy Eα = 9709±50 keV and a lifetime (time dif-
                    ference between implantation of the evaporation residue and the α decay) of τ = 165 ms were observed.
                    From the latter a half-life T1/2 = 115+231
                                                          −75 ms was extracted. Due to the signiﬁcantly diﬀerent half-lives
                    it was speculated that the Eα = 9709±50 keV decay represents the ground state decay, the events of
                    Eα = 10376±35 keV represent the decay of an isomeric state [186]. Decay data of higher quality were
                    obtained in succeeding experiments, either producing 262 Bh directly in the reaction 209 Bi(54 Cr,1n)262 Bh
                    [164, 186, 190] or by α decay of 266 Mt [187, 189, 197]. In these experiments the existence of an iso-
                    meric state was secured, but it turned out, that the α decay spectrum of 262m,262g Bh has a complicated
                    structure ranging from Eα ≈ 9.6 MeV to Eα ≈ 10.75 MeV, and decays of 262g Bh and 262m Bh over-
                    lay in energy. The SHIP results were conﬁrmed by C.M. Folden III at the BGS, LBNL Berkeley [191]
                    and by T. Zhao et al. at the SHANS 2 separator at IMP, Lanzhou [192]. The data are compared in table 7.

                         In the following the decay of 262m,262g Bh will be discussed in some more detail. For this purpose the
                    data given in [186, 187, 189, 190, 197, 191, 164, 192] are merged.
                    The energy distributions for all events from the diﬀerent experiments registered with full energy release
                    in the ’stop detectors’ and attributed to the decay of 262 Bh are shown in ﬁg. 37a. It should be noted
                    that the α energies might be inﬂuenced by energy summing with CE and thus not represent transition
                    energies between distinct levels. Nevertheless they can be divided into three groups on the basis of the
                    lifetimes as shown in ﬁg. 37b.
                    a) The events in group 1 have half-lives of T1/2 > 60 ms, and are found essentially in the energy range
                    Eα < 10 MeV. Evidently they represent decays of the activity denoted as 262 Bh(1) [164].
                    b) The events in group 2 have half-lives of T1/2 < 22 ms, and are found essentially in the energy range
                    Eα > 10.15 MeV. Evidently they represent decays of the activity denoted as 262 Bh(2) [164].
                    c) The events in group 3 have half-lives T1/2 = (40-60) ms, probably a mixture of decays of 262 Bh(1) and
                    262
                        Bh(2). They are not considered in the further discussion.

the correlated 258 Db decays is shown in ﬁg. 38. Three lines are visible.
a) Eα = 9186 ± 3 keV, T1/2 = 1.12+0.73 −0.32 s.
